\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\left|\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\right|}\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\times B_{1}}u(y)\,d{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}(\omega)\,dy=\int_{B_{1}}u(y)\,dy>0,

and therefore
u_{\sharp}
does not vanish identically.

As a consequence, we can define

u_{\star}:=\frac{u_{\sharp}}{\|u_{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}}.

We know that
u_{\star}\in H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})
, due to (
4.37
).
Moreover, in view of Lemma
4.5
,

(-\Delta)^{s}u_{\star}=\frac{(-\Delta)^{s}u_{\sharp}}{\|u_{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}}=\frac{\big((-\Delta)^{s}u\big)_{\sharp}}{\|u_{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}}=\frac{\lambda_{1}\,u_{\sharp}}{\|u_{\sharp}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1})}}=\lambda_{1}\,u_{\star},

which gives the desired result.
∎

Now, we are in the position of proving the following result.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
s\geq 1
and
r\in(0,1)
. If
u\in H_{0}^{s}\left(B_{1}\right)
and
u
is radial, then
u\in C^{\alpha}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{r}\right)
for any
\alpha\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
.

Proof.

We write

u\left(x\right)=u_{0}\left(\left|x\right|\right),\qquad\mbox{for some }\;u_{0}:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

(4.38)

and we observe that
u\in H_{0}^{s}\left(B_{1}\right)\subset H^{1}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\right)
.

Accordingly, for any
0<r<1
, we have

\infty>\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{r}}|u(x)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{r}^{+\infty}|u_{0}(\rho)|^{2}\rho^{n-1}\,d\rho\geq r^{n-1}\int_{r}^{+\infty}|u_{0}(\rho)|^{2}\,d\rho

(4.39)

and

\infty>\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{r}}{|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\,dx}=\int_{r}^{+\infty}{\left|u_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\right|^{2}\rho^{n-1}\,d\rho}\geq r^{n-1}\int_{r}^{+\infty}{\left|u_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\right|^{2}\,d\rho}.

(4.40)

Thanks to (
4.39
) and (
4.40
) we have that
u_{0}\in H^{1}\left(\left(r,+\infty\right)\right)
,
with
u_{0}=0
in
[1,+\infty)
.

Then, from
the Morrey Embedding Theorem, it follows that

u_{0}\in C^{\alpha}\left(\left(r,+\infty\right)\right)
for any

\alpha\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
, which leads to the desired result.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
s\in(0,+\infty)
.
There exists a radial, nonnegative
and nontrivial solution of (
4.32
)
which belongs to
H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})\cap C^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2})
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

If
s\in(0,1)
, the desired claim follows from
Corollary 8 in
[
DSV1
]
.

If instead
s\geq 1
, we obtain the desired result as a consequence of
Proposition
4.7
and Lemma
4.8
.
∎

4.3
Boundary asymptotics of the first eigenfunctions of
(-\Delta)^{s}

In Lemma 4 of
[
DSV1
]
, some precise asymptotics
at the boundary for the first Dirichlet eigenfunction
of
(-\Delta)^{s}
have been established in the range
s\in(0,1)
.

Here, we obtain a related expansion in the range
s>0

for the eigenfunction provided in Corollary
4.9
.
The result that we obtain is the following:

Proposition 4.10
.

There exists a nontrivial solution
\phi_{*}
of (
4.32
)
which belongs to
H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})\cap C^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2})
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and such that, for every
e\in\partial B_{1}

and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{\left|\beta\right|-s}\partial^{\beta}\phi_{*}\left(e+\epsilon X\right)=\left(-1\right)^{\left|\beta\right|}k_{*}\,s\left(s-1\right)\ldots\left(s-\left|\beta\right|+1\right)e_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\ldots e_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\left(-e\cdot X\right)_{+}^{s-\left|\beta\right|},

in the sense of distribution, with
|\beta|:=\beta_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n}

and
k_{*}>0
.

The proof of Proposition
4.10
relies on Proposition
4.2

and some auxiliary computations on the Green function in (
4.1
).
We start with the following result:

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
0<r<1
,
e\in\partial B_{1}
,
s>0
,

f\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
f=0
outside
B_{1}
. Then the integral

\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz

(4.41)

is finite.

Proof.

We denote by
I
the integral in (
4.41
). We let

I_{1}:=\int_{B_{1}\setminus B_{r}}f(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz\qquad{\mbox{and}}\qquad I_{2}:=\int_{B_{r}}f(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz.

Then, we have that

I=I_{1}+I_{2}.

(4.42)

Now, if
z\in B_{1}\setminus B_{r}
, we have that

f(z)\leq|f(z)-f(e)|\leq C|z-e|^{\alpha},

therefore

I_{1}\leq\int_{B_{1}\setminus B_{r}}\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n-\alpha}}\,dz<\infty.

(4.43)

If instead
z\in B_{r}
,

|z-e|\geq 1-r>0,

and consequently

I_{2}\leq\frac{1}{s\,(1-r)^{n}}\int_{B_{r}}f(z)\,dz<\infty.

(4.44)

The desired result follows from (
4.42
),
(
4.43
) and (
4.44
).
∎

Next result gives
a precise boundary behaviour of the Green function
for any
s>0
(the case in which
s\in(0,1)
and
f\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
was considered in Lemma 6 of
[
DSV1
]
,
and in fact the proof presented here also simplifies the one in
Lemma 6 of
[
DSV1
]
for the setting considered there).

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
e
,
\omega\in\partial B_{1}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
r\in(0,1)
.
Assume that

e+\epsilon\omega\in B_{1},

(4.45)

for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
. Let
f\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, with
f=0
outside
B_{1}
.

Then

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)\,dz=k(n,s)\,\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\frac{(-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz,

(4.46)

for a suitable normalizing constant
k(n,s)>0
.

Proof.

In light of (
4.45
), we have that

1>|e+\epsilon\omega|^{2}=1+\epsilon^{2}+2\epsilon e\cdot\omega,

and therefore

-e\cdot\omega>\frac{\epsilon}{2}>0.

(4.47)

Moreover, if
r_{0}
is as given in (
4.1
), we have that, for all
z\in B_{1}
,

r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)=\frac{\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)(1-|z|^{2})}{|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}}\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}}.

(4.48)

Also, a Taylor series representation allows us to write, for any
t\in(-1,1)
,

\frac{t^{s-1}}{(t+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{-n/2}{k}t^{k+s-1}.

(4.49)

We also notice that

\begin{split}&\left|\binom{-n/2}{k}\right|=\left|\frac{-\frac{n}{2}\left(-\frac{n}{2}-1\right)\,...\,\left(-\frac{n}{2}-k+1\right)}{k!}\right|=\frac{\frac{n}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}+1\right)\,...\,\left(\frac{n}{2}+(k-1)\right)}{k!}\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{n\left(n+1\right)\,...\,\left(n+(k-1)\right)}{k!}\leq\frac{\left(n+(k-1)\right)!}{k!}=(k+1)\,...\,\left(n+(k-1)\right)\\
&\qquad\leq(n+k+1)^{n+1}.\end{split}

(4.50)

This and the Root Test
give that the series in (
4.49
)
is uniformly convergent on compact sets in
(-1,1)
.

As a consequence, if we set

r_{1}(x,z):=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},r_{0}(x,z)\right\},

(4.51)

we can switch integration and summation signs and obtain that

\int_{0}^{r_{1}(x,z)}\frac{t^{s-1}}{(t+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,dt=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(r_{1}(x,z))^{k+s},

(4.52)

where

c_{k}:=\frac{1}{k+s}\binom{-n/2}{k}.

Once again, the bound in (
4.50
), together with (
4.51
),
give that the series in (
4.52
) is convergent.

Now, we omit for simplicity the normalizing constant
k(n,s)

in the definition of the Green function in (
4.1
),
and
we define

\mathcal{G}(x,z):=|z-x|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}(r_{1}(x,z))^{k+s}

(4.53)

and

g(x,z):=|z-x|^{2s-n}\int_{r_{1}(x,z)}^{r_{0}(x,z)}\frac{t^{s-1}}{(t+1)^{\frac{n}{2}}}\,dt.

Using (
4.1
)
and (
4.52
), and dropping dimensional constants for the sake of shortness,
we can write

\mathcal{G}_{s}(x,z)=\mathcal{G}(x,z)+g(x,z).

(4.54)

Now, we show that

g(x,z)\leq\begin{cases}C\chi(r,z)\,|z-x|^{2s-n}&\quad\text{if}\quad n>2s,\\
C\chi(r,z)\,\log r_{0}(x,z)&\quad\text{if}\quad n=2s,\\
C\chi(r,z)\,|z-x|^{2s-n}(r_{0}(x,z))^{s-\frac{n}{2}}&\quad\text{if}\quad n<2s,\end{cases}

(4.55)

where
\chi(r,z)=1
if
r_{0}(x,z)>\frac{1}{2}

and
\chi(r,z)=0
if
r_{0}(x,z)\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
To check this,
we notice that if
r_{0}(x,z)\leq\frac{1}{2}
we have that
r_{1}(x,z)=r_{0}(x,z)
,
due to (
4.51
), and therefore
g(x,z)=0
.

On the other hand, if
r_{0}(x,z)>\frac{1}{2}
,
we deduce from (
4.51
) that
r_{1}(x,z)=\frac{1}{2}
, and consequently

g(x,z)\leq|z-x|^{2s-n}\int_{1/2}^{r_{0}(x,z)}t^{s-\frac{n}{2}-1}dt\leq\begin{cases}C|z-x|^{2s-n}&\quad\text{if}\quad n>2s,\\
C\log r_{0}(x,z)&\quad\text{if}\quad n=2s,\\
C|z-x|^{2s-n}(r_{0}(x,z))^{s-\frac{n}{2}}&\quad\text{if}\quad n<2s,\end{cases}

for some constant
C>0
. This completes the proof of (
4.55
).

Now, we exploit the bound in (
4.55
) when
x=e+\epsilon\omega
.
For this, we notice that
if
r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)>\frac{1}{2}
,
recalling (
4.48
), we find that

|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}\leq 6\epsilon<9\epsilon,

(4.56)

and therefore
z\in B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)
.

Hence, using (
4.55
),

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{B_{1}}f(z)g(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|\leq\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|f(z)||g(e+\epsilon\omega,z)|dz\\
&\leq\begin{cases}C\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|f(z)||z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n>2s,\\
C\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|f(z)|\log r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n=2s,\\
C\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|f(z)||z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}(r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{s-\frac{n}{2}}dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n<2s.\end{cases}\end{split}

(4.57)

Now, if
z\in B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)
,
then

|z-e|\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|+|\epsilon\omega|\leq 3\sqrt{\epsilon}+\epsilon<4\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(4.58)

Furthermore,
for a given
r\in(0,1)
, we have that
B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{r}
, provided that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small.

Hence, if
z\in B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)
,
we can exploit the regularity of
f
and deduce that

|f(z)|=|f(z)-f(e)|\leq C|z-e|^{\alpha}.

This and (
4.58
) lead to

|f(z)|\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},

(4.59)

for every
z\in B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)
.

Thanks to (
4.48
), (
4.57
) and (
4.59
), we have that

\begin{split}\left|\int_{B_{1}}f(z)g(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|&\leq\begin{cases}C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n>2s,\\
C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}\log\frac{3\epsilon}{|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}}dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n=2s,\\
C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+s-\frac{n}{2}}\displaystyle\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}dz&\quad\text{if}\quad n<2s\end{cases}\\
&\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+s},\end{split}

up to renaming
C
.

This and (
4.54
) give that

\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz=\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz+o(\epsilon^{s}).

(4.60)

Now, we consider the series in (
4.53
),
and we split the contribution coming from the index
k=0
from the ones coming from the indices
k>0
, namely
we write

\begin{split}&\mathcal{G}(x,z)=\mathcal{G}_{0}(x,z)+\mathcal{G}_{1}(x,z),\\
\quad\text{with}\quad&\mathcal{G}_{0}(x,z):=\frac{|z-x|^{2s-n}}{s}(r_{1}(x,z))^{s}\\
\quad\text{and}\quad&\mathcal{G}_{1}(x,z):=|z-x|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}c_{k}\,(r_{1}(x,z))^{k+s}.\end{split}

(4.61)

Firstly, we consider the contribution given by the term
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Thanks to (
4.51
) and (
4.59
), we have that

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{B_{1}\cap B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|\leq\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|f(z)|\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{k+s}dz\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k+s}dz\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}dz\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+s},\end{split}

(4.62)

up to renaming the constant
C
step by step.

On the other hand, for every
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

|z|=|e+\epsilon\omega+z-e-\epsilon\omega|\geq|e+\epsilon\omega|-|z-e-\epsilon\omega|\geq 1-\epsilon-|z-e-\epsilon\omega|.

Therefore,
for every
z\in B_{1}\setminus\left(B_{r}\cup B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)\right)
, we can take
e_{*}:=\frac{z}{|z|}
and obtain that

\begin{split}&|f(z)|=|f(z)-f(e_{*})|\leq C|z-e_{*}|^{\alpha}=C(1-|z|)^{\alpha}\\
&\qquad\leq C(\epsilon+|z-e-\epsilon\omega|)^{\alpha}\leq C|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\alpha},\end{split}

(4.63)

up to renaming
C>0
.

Also, using (
4.48
), we see that, for any
k>0
,

\begin{split}&(r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\leq\frac{C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}}{2^{k}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s+\frac{\alpha}{2}}}.\end{split}

(4.64)

This, (
4.51
) and (
4.63
) give that if
z\in B_{1}\setminus\left(B_{r}\cup B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)\right)
, then

\begin{split}|f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)|\,&\leq C|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\alpha+2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{k+s}\\
&=C|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\alpha+2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{k-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&\leq C|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\alpha+2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,(r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z))^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&\leq C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{|c_{k}|}{2^{k}},\end{split}

where the latter series is absolutely convergent thanks to (
4.50
).

This implies that, if we
set
E:=B_{1}\setminus\left(B_{r}\cup B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)\right)
, it holds that

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{E}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|\leq C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\int_{E}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-n}dz\\
\quad\quad&\qquad\qquad\leq C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\int_{B_{1}}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-n}dz\leq C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\int_{B_{3}}|z|^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-n}dz\leq C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}.\end{split}

(4.65)

Moreover, if
z\in B_{r}
, we have that

|e+\epsilon\omega-z|\geq 1-\epsilon-r,

and therefore, recalling (
4.64
),

\begin{split}\sup_{z\in B_{r}}|\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)|\,&\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,\big(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)\big)^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\big(r_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)\big)^{k-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}|c_{k}|\,\big(r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)\big)^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&\leq C\,|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{-n-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{|c_{k}|}{2^{k}}\\
&\leq C(1-\epsilon-r)^{-n-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\,\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}},\end{split}

up to renaming
C
.

As a consequence, we find that

\begin{split}\left|\int_{B_{r}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|&\leq\sup_{z\in B_{r}}|\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)|\,\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}\\
&\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}(1-\epsilon-r)^{-n-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}2^{n+\frac{\alpha}{2}}(1-r)^{-n-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}}\\
&=C\epsilon^{s+\frac{\alpha}{4}},\end{split}

(4.66)

as long as
\epsilon
is suitably
small with respect to
r
, and
C
is a positive constant
which depends on
\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}
,
r
,
n
and
\alpha
.

Then, by (
4.62
), (
4.65
) and (
4.66
) we conclude that

\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz=o(\epsilon^{s}).

(4.67)

Inserting this information into (
4.60
),
and recalling (
4.61
),
we obtain

\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz=\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz+o(\epsilon^{s}).

(4.68)

Now, we define

\mathcal{D}_{1}:=\left\{z\in B_{1}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)>1/2\right\}

and

\mathcal{D}_{2}:=\left\{z\in B_{1}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)\leq 1/2\right\}.

If
z\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then
z\in B_{1}\setminus B_{r}
, thanks to (
4.56
),
and hence we can use (
4.57
) and (
4.59
) and write

|f(z)\mathcal{G}_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)|\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}.

Then, recalling again (
4.57
),

\left|\int_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{1}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\right|\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\int_{B_{3\sqrt{\epsilon}}(e+\epsilon\omega)}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}dz=C\epsilon^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+s},

(4.69)

up to renaming the constant
C>0
. This information and (
4.68
) give that

\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz+o(\epsilon^{s}).

Now, by (
4.48
) and (
4.51
), if
z\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
,

\mathcal{G}_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)=\frac{|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2s-n}}{s}(r_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega))^{s}=\frac{\epsilon^{s}(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n}}.

Hence, we have

\begin{split}&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\\
=\;&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{0}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz\\
=\;&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{\left\{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)(1-|z|^{2})\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}\right\}}f(z)\frac{(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n}}dz.\end{split}

(4.70)

Now we set

F_{\epsilon}(z):=\begin{cases}f(z)\displaystyle\frac{(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n}}&\quad\text{if}\quad 2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)(1-|z|^{2})\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2},\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(4.71)

and we prove that for any
\eta>0
there exists
\delta>0
independent
of
\epsilon
such that, for any
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|E|\leq\delta
, we have

\int_{B_{1}\cap E}|F_{\epsilon}(z)|dz\leq\eta.

(4.72)

To this aim, given
\eta
and
E
as above,
we define

\rho:=\min\left\{\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega),\,\sqrt{{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}(1-r)},\,\left(\frac{2^{s+\alpha}s^{2}\,\epsilon^{s+\alpha}\,(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{\alpha}\eta}{3^{2s}\,\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}\,|\partial B_{1}|}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\alpha}}\right\}.

(4.73)

We stress that the above definition is well-posed, thanks to (
4.47
).
In addition, using the integrability of
f
, we take
\delta>0

such that if
A\subseteq B_{1}
and
|A|\leq\delta
then

\int_{A}|f(x)|\,dx\leq\frac{s\rho^{n}\eta}{2\cdot 3^{s}}.

(4.74)

We set

E_{1}:=E\cap B_{\rho}(e+\epsilon\omega)\qquad{\mbox{and}}\qquad E_{2}:=E\setminus B_{\rho}(e+\epsilon\omega).

(4.75)

From (
4.71
), we see that

|F_{\epsilon}(z)|\leq\frac{|f(z)|\,\chi_{\star}(z)}{2^{s}s\,\epsilon^{s}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n-2s}},

where

\chi_{\star}(z):=\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{if}\quad 2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)(1-|z|^{2})\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2},\\
0&\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and therefore

\int_{B_{1}\cap E_{1}}|F_{\epsilon}(z)|\,dz\leq\int_{B_{1}\cap E_{1}}\frac{|f(z)|\,\chi_{\star}(z)}{2^{s}s\,\epsilon^{s}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n-2s}}\,dz.

(4.76)

Now, for every
z\in B_{1}\cap E_{1}\subseteq B_{\rho}(e+\epsilon\omega)
for which
\chi_{\star}(z)\neq 0
,
we have that

2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)(1-|z|^{2})\leq|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{2}\leq\rho^{2},

and hence

|z|\geq\sqrt{1-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}}\geq 1-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)},

which in turn gives that
|z|\geq r
, recall (
4.73
).

From this and (
4.76
) we deduce that

\begin{split}&\int_{B_{1}\cap E_{1}}|F_{\epsilon}(z)|\,dz\leq\int_{1-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}\leq|z|<1}\frac{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}\,(1-|z|)^{\alpha}}{2^{s}s\,\epsilon^{s}|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n-2s}}\,dz\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}}{2^{s}s\,\epsilon^{s}}\,\left(\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}\right)^{\alpha}\int_{1-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}\leq|z|<1}\frac{dz}{|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n-2s}}\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}}{2^{s}s\,\epsilon^{s}}\,\left(\frac{\rho^{2}}{2\epsilon(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)}\right)^{\alpha}\int_{B_{3}}\frac{dx}{|x|^{n-2s}}\\
&\qquad=\frac{3^{2s}\,\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}\,|\partial B_{1}|}{2^{s+\alpha+1}s^{2}\,\epsilon^{s+\alpha}\,(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{\alpha}}\,\;\rho^{2\alpha}\\
&\qquad\leq\frac{\eta}{2},\end{split}

(4.77)

where (
4.73
) has been exploited in the last inequality.

We also point out that, by (
4.71
),
(
4.74
) and (
4.75
),

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}\cap E_{2}}|F_{\epsilon}(z)|\,dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{(B_{1}\setminus B_{\rho}(e+\epsilon\omega))\cap E}|f(z)|\,\frac{(-\epsilon-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e-\epsilon\omega|^{n}}\,dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{3^{s}}{s\rho^{n}}\int_{B_{1}\cap E}|f(z)|\,dz

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{2}.

This, (
4.75
) and (
4.77
) give (
4.72
),
as desired.

Notice also that the sequence
F_{\epsilon}(z)
converges pointwise
to the function

F(z):=f(z)\frac{(-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}.

Hence (
4.70
), (
4.72
) and the Vitali Convergence Theorem allow us to conclude that

\begin{split}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\mathcal{G}_{s}(e+\epsilon\omega,z)dz&=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{B_{1}}F_{\epsilon}(z)dz\\
&=\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\frac{(-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}dz,\end{split}

(4.78)

which establishes the claim of Lemma
4.12

(notice that the finiteness of the latter quantity in (
4.78
)
follows from (
4.11
)).
∎

With this preliminary work,
we can now establish the boundary behaviour of solutions which is needed
in our setting. As a matter of fact, from Lemma
4.12
we immediately deduce that:

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
e
,
\omega\in\partial B_{1}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0

and
r\in(0,1)
.

Assume that
e+\epsilon\omega\in B_{1}
, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
. Let
f\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{r})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, with
f=0
outside
B_{1}
.

Let
u
be as in (
4.19
).
Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}u(e+\epsilon\omega)=k(n,s)(-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}\int_{B_{1}}f(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}dz,

where
k(n,s)
denotes a positive normalizing constant.

Now we apply the previous results to detect the
boundary growth of a suitable
first eigenfunction. For our purposes, the statement that we need
is the following:

Corollary 4.14
.

There exists a nontrivial solution
\phi_{*}
of (
4.32
)
which belongs to
H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})\cap C^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2})
,
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and such that, for every
e\in\partial B_{1}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon\omega)=k_{*}\,(-e\cdot\omega)^{s}_{+},

(4.79)

for a suitable constant
k_{*}>0
.

Furthermore,
for every
R\in(r,1)
, there exists
C_{R}>0
such that

\sup_{x\in B_{1}\setminus B_{R}}d^{-s}(x)\,|\phi_{*}(x)|\leq C_{R}.

(4.80)

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\phi\in H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})\cap C^{\alpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2})

be the nonnegative
and nontrivial solution of (
4.32
), as given in Corollary
4.9
.

In the spirit of (
4.19
),
we define

\phi_{*}(x):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\int_{B_{1}}\mathcal{G}_{s}\left(x,y\right)\,\phi(y)\,dy&{\mbox{ if }}x\in B_{1},\\
0&{\mbox{ if }}x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1}.\end{cases}

We stress that we can use Proposition
4.2

in this context, with
f:=\lambda_{1}\phi
,
since condition (
4.18
) is satisfied in this case.

Then, from (
4.20
) and (
4.22
), we know that
\phi_{*}\in H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})

and, from (
4.21
),

(-\Delta)^{s}\phi_{*}=\lambda_{1}\,\phi{\mbox{ in }}B_{1}.

In particular, we have that
(-\Delta)^{s}(\phi-\phi_{*})=0
in
B_{1}
,
and
\phi-\phi_{*}\in H^{s}_{0}(B_{1})
, which give that
\phi-\phi_{*}
vanishes identically.
Hence, we can write that
\phi=\phi_{*}
, and thus
\phi_{*}

is a solution of (
4.32
).

Now, we check (
4.79
).
For this, we distinguish two cases.
If
e\cdot\omega\geq 0
, we have that

|e+\epsilon\omega|^{2}=1+2\epsilon e\cdot\omega+\epsilon^{2}>1,

for all
\epsilon>0
. Then, in this case
e+\epsilon\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1}
, and therefore
\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon\omega)=0
.
This gives that, in this case,

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon\omega)=0.

(4.81)

If instead
e\cdot\omega<0
, we see that

|e+\epsilon\omega|^{2}=1+2\epsilon e\cdot\omega+\epsilon^{2}<1,

for all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small. Hence, we can exploit
Corollary
4.13
and find that

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon\omega)=\lambda_{1}\,k(n,s)(-2e\cdot\omega)^{s}\int_{B_{1}}\phi(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz,

(4.82)

with
k(n,s)>0
. Then, we define

k_{*}:=2^{s}\,k(n,s)\int_{B_{1}}\phi(z)\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{s}}{s|z-e|^{n}}\,dz.

We observe that
k_{*}
is positive by construction,
with
k(n,s)>0
. Also, in light of
Lemma
4.11
, we know that
k_{*}
is finite.
Hence, from (
4.81
) and (
4.82
)
we obtain (
4.79
), as desired.

It only remains to check (
4.80
).
For this, we use (
4.21
), and we see that,
for every
R\in(r,1)
,

\sup_{x\in B_{1}\setminus B_{R}}d^{-s}(x)\,|\phi_{*}(x)|\leq C_{R}\,\lambda_{1}\,\big(\|\phi\|_{L^{1}(B_{1})}+\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}\setminus B_{r})}\big),

and this gives (
4.80
) up to renaming
C_{R}
.
∎

Now, we can complete the proof of Proposition
4.10
, by arguing as follows.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

Let
\psi
be a test function in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let also
R:=\frac{r+1}{2}\in(r,1)
and

g_{\epsilon}(X):=\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\partial^{\beta}\psi(X).

We claim that

\sup_{{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}|g_{\epsilon}(X)|\leq C,

(4.83)

for some
C>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
To prove this, we distinguish three cases.
If
e+\epsilon X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1}
,
we have that
\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)=0
and thus
g_{\epsilon}(X)=0
.
If instead
e+\epsilon X\in B_{R}
,
we observe that

R>|e+\epsilon X|\geq 1-\epsilon|X|,

and therefore
|X|\geq\frac{1-R}{\epsilon}
. In particular,
in this case
X
falls outside the support of
\psi
, as long as
\epsilon>0

is sufficiently small, and consequently
\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)=0

and
g_{\epsilon}(X)=0
.

Hence, to complete the proof of (
4.83
),
we are only left with the case in which
e+\epsilon X\in B_{1}\setminus B_{R}
. In this situation,
we make use of (
4.80
) and we find that

\displaystyle|\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)|\leq C\,d^{s}(e+\epsilon X)=C\,(1-|e+\epsilon X|)^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\,(1-|e+\epsilon X|)^{s}(1+|e+\epsilon X|)^{s}=C\,(1-|e+\epsilon X|^{2})^{s}

\displaystyle\qquad=C\,\epsilon^{s}(-2e\cdot X-\epsilon|X|^{2})^{s}\leq C\epsilon^{s},

for some
C>0
possibly varying from line to line,
and this completes the proof of (
4.83
).

Now, from (
4.83
) and the
Dominated Convergence Theorem, we obtain that

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX.

(4.84)

On the other hand, by Corollary
4.14
,
used here with
\omega:=\frac{X}{|X|}
, we know that

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon|X|\omega)=|X|^{s}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon\omega)

\displaystyle\qquad=k_{*}\,|X|^{s}\,(-e\cdot\omega)^{s}_{+}=k_{*}\,(-e\cdot X)^{s}_{+}.

Substituting this into (
4.84
), we thus find that

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX=k_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-e\cdot X)^{s}_{+}\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX.

As a consequence, integrating by parts twice,

\begin{split}&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{|\beta|-s}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial^{\beta}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\psi(X)dX=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial^{\beta}\Big(\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\Big)\psi(X)dX\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{|\beta|}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\epsilon^{-s}\phi_{*}(e+\epsilon X)\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{|\beta|}\,k_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-e\cdot X)^{s}_{+}\partial^{\beta}\psi(X)dX\\
&\qquad=k_{*}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\partial^{\beta}(-e\cdot X)^{s}_{+}\psi(X)dX\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{|\beta|}\,k_{*}\,s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\beta|+1)e_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\ldots e_{n}^{\beta_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(-e\cdot X)^{s-|\beta|}_{+}\psi(X)dX.\end{split}

Since the test function
\psi
is arbitrary, the claim in
Proposition
4.10
is proved.
∎

4.4
Boundary behaviour of
s
-harmonic functions

In this section we analyze the asymptotic behaviour of
s
-harmonic
functions, with a “spherical bump function” as exterior Dirichlet datum.

The result needed for our purpose is the following:

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
s>0
. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

and
\sigma\in(0,1)
such that
s=m+\sigma
.

Then, there exists

\psi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
(-\Delta)^{s}\psi=0
in
B_{1}
,

(4.85)

and, for
every
x\in\partial B_{1-\epsilon}
,

\psi(x)=k\,\epsilon^{s}+o(\epsilon^{s}),

(4.86)

as
\epsilon\searrow 0
, for some
k>0
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\,[0,1])
such that
\overline{\psi}=0
in
\mathbb{R}\setminus(2,3)
and
\overline{\psi}>0
in
(2,3)
. Let
\psi_{0}(x):=(-1)^{m}\overline{\psi}(|x|)
.
We recall the Poisson kernel

\Gamma_{s}(x,y):=(-1)^{m}\frac{\gamma_{n,\sigma}}{|x-y|^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{s}_{+}}{(|y|^{2}-1)^{s}},

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\overline{B_{1}}
, and
a suitable normalization constant
\gamma_{n,\sigma}>0
(see formulas (1.10) and (1.30)
in
[
ABX
]
).
We define

\psi(x):=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1}}\Gamma_{s}(x,y)\,\psi_{0}(y)\,dy+\psi_{0}(x).

Notice that
\psi_{0}=0
in
B_{3/2}
and therefore we can
exploit Theorem
in
[
ABX
]
and obtain that (
4.85
)
is satisfied
(notice also that
\psi=\psi_{0}
outside
B_{1}
, hence
\psi
is compactly supported).

Furthermore, to prove (
4.86
)
we borrow some ideas from Lemma 2.2 in
[
MR3626547
]

and we see that, for any
x\in\partial B_{1-\epsilon}
,

\begin{split}\psi(x)&=c(-1)^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{1}}\frac{\psi_{0}(y)(1-|x|^{2})^{s}}{(|y|^{2}-1)^{s}|x-y|^{n}}dy+\psi_{0}(x)\\
&=c(-1)^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{1}}\frac{\psi_{0}(y)(1-|x|^{2})^{s}}{(|y|^{2}-1)^{s}|x-y|^{n}}dy\\
&=c\,(1-|x|^{2})^{s}\int_{2}^{3}\left[\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{\rho^{n-1}\overline{\psi}(\rho)}{(\rho^{2}-1)^{s}|x-\rho\omega|^{n}}d\omega\right]d\rho\\
&=c\,(2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2})^{s}\int_{2}^{3}\left[\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{\rho^{n-1}\overline{\psi}(\rho)}{(\rho^{2}-1)^{s}|(1-\epsilon)e_{1}-\rho\omega|^{n}}d\omega\right]d\rho\\
&=2^{s}c\,\epsilon^{s}\int_{2}^{3}\left[\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{\rho^{n-1}\overline{\psi}(\rho)}{(\rho^{2}-1)^{s}|e_{1}-\rho\omega|^{n}}d\omega\right]d\rho+o(\epsilon^{s})\\
&=c\epsilon^{s}+o(\epsilon^{s}),\end{split}

where
c>0
is a constant possibly varying from line to line, and this establishes (
4.86
).
∎

Remark 4.16
.

As in Proposition
4.10
, one can extend (
4.86
)
to higher derivatives (in the distributional sense), obtaining, for any
e\in\partial B_{1}

and
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}

\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{|\beta|-s}\partial^{\beta}\psi(e+\epsilon X)=k_{\beta}\,e_{1}^{\beta_{1}}\dots e_{n}^{\beta_{n}}(-e\cdot X)_{+}^{s-|\beta|},

for some
\kappa_{\beta}\neq 0
.

Using Lemma
4.15
, in the spirit of
[
MR3626547
]
, we
can construct a sequence of
s
-harmonic functions
approaching
(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}
for a fixed unit vector
e
,
by using a blow-up argument. Namely, we prove the following:

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
e\in\partial B_{1}
. There exists a sequence
v_{e,j}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
(-\Delta)^{s}v_{e,j}=0
in
B_{1}(e)
,
v_{e,j}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{4j}(e)
, and

v_{e,j}\to\kappa(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}\quad\mbox{in}\quad L^{1}(B_{1}(e)),

as
j\to+\infty
, for some
\kappa>0
.

Proof.

Let
\psi
be as in Lemma
4.15
and
define

v_{e,j}(x):=j^{s}\psi\left(\frac{x}{j}-e\right).

The
s
-harmonicity and the property of being compactly supported follow
by the ones of
\psi
. We now prove the convergence.
To this aim, given
x\in B_{1}(e)
, we write
p_{j}:=\frac{x}{j}-e
and
\epsilon_{j}:=1-|p_{j}|
. Recall that since
x\in B_{1}(e)
, then
|x-e|^{2}<1
, which implies that
|x|^{2}<2x\cdot e
and
x\cdot e>0
for any
x\in B_{1}(e)
.

As a consequence

|p_{j}|^{2}=\left|\frac{x}{j}-e\right|^{2}=\frac{|x|^{2}}{j^{2}}+1-2\frac{x}{j}\cdot e=1-\frac{2}{j}(x\cdot e)_{+}+o\left(\frac{1}{j}\right)(x\cdot e)^{2}_{+},

and so

\epsilon_{j}=\frac{(1+o(1))}{j}(x\cdot e)_{+}.

Therefore, using (
4.86
),

\begin{split}v_{e,j}(x)&=j^{s}\psi(p_{j})\\
&=j^{s}\kappa(\epsilon_{j}^{s}+o(\epsilon^{s}_{j}))\\
&=j^{s}\left(\frac{\kappa}{j^{s}}(x\cdot e)_{+}^{s}+o\left(\frac{1}{j^{s}}\right)\right)\\
&=\kappa(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}+o(1).\end{split}

Integrating over
B_{1}(e)
, we obtain the desired
L^{1}
-convergence.
∎

Now, we show that, as in the case
s\in(0,1)
proved in
Theorem 3.1 of
[
MR3626547
]
, we can find an
s
-harmonic function
with an arbitrarily large number of derivatives prescribed at some point.

Proposition 4.18
.

For any
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, there exist
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
R>r>0
, and
v\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\begin{cases}(-\Delta)^{s}v=0&\quad\text{in}\quad B_{r}(p),\\
v=0&\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{R}(p),\end{cases}

(4.87)

D^{\alpha}v(p)=0\quad{\mbox{ for any }}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\quad{\mbox{ with }}\;|\alpha|\leq|\beta|-1,

D^{\alpha}v(p)=0\quad{\mbox{ for any }}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\quad{\mbox{ with }}\;|\alpha|=|\beta|\quad{\mbox{ and }}\;\alpha\neq\beta

and

D^{\beta}v(p)=1.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the set of all pairs
(v,x)\in\left(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)\times B_{r}(p)
that satisfy (
4.87
) for some
R>r>0
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

To each pair
(v,x)\in\mathcal{Z}
we associate the vector

\left(D^{\alpha}v(x)\right)_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
, for some
K^{\prime}=K^{\prime}_{|\beta|}
and
consider
\mathcal{V}
to be the vector space spanned by this construction, namely
we set

\mathcal{V}:=\Big\{\left(D^{\alpha}v(x)\right)_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|},\quad{\mbox{ with }}\;(v,x)\in\mathcal{Z}\Big\}.

We claim that

\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}.

(4.88)

To check this, we suppose by contradiction that
\mathcal{V}
lies in a
proper subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
. Then,
\mathcal{V}
must lie in a
hyperplane, hence there exists

c=(c_{\alpha})_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}\setminus\left\{0\right\}

(4.89)

which is orthogonal to any vector
\left(D^{\alpha}v(x)\right)_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}

with
(v,x)\in\mathcal{Z}
, that is

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}v(x)=0.

(4.90)

We notice that the pair
(v_{e,j},x)
, with
v_{j}
as in Corollary
4.17
,

e\in\partial B_{1}

and
x\in B_{1}(e)
, belongs to
\mathcal{Z}
. Consequently,
fixed
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2}

and set
e:=\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}
, we have that (
4.90
) holds true when
v:=v_{e,j}
and
x\in B_{1}(e)
, namely

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}v(x)=0.

Let now
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B_{1}(e))
.
Integrating by parts,
by Corollary
4.17
and the Dominated Convergence Theorem,
we have that

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}v_{e,j}(x)\varphi(x)\,dx=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}(-1)^{|\alpha|}c_{\alpha}v_{e,j}(x)D^{\alpha}\varphi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}(-1)^{|\alpha|}c_{\alpha}(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}D^{\alpha}\varphi(x)\,dx=\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}\varphi(x)\,dx.

This gives that, for every
x\in B_{1}(e)
,

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}=0.

Moreover, for every
x\in B_{1}(e)
,

D^{\alpha}(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}=s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\alpha|+1)(x\cdot e)^{s-|\alpha|}_{+}e_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots e_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}.

In particular, for
x=\frac{e}{|\xi|}\in B_{1}(e)
,

D^{\alpha}(x\cdot e)^{s}_{+}\big|_{|_{x=e/{|\xi|}}}=s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\alpha|+1)|\xi|^{-s}\xi_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\xi_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}.

And, using the usual multi-index notation, we write

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|\beta|}c_{\alpha}s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\alpha|+1)\xi^{\alpha}=0,

(4.91)

for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B_{1/2}
.
The identity (
4.91
) describes
a polynomial in
\xi
which vanishes for any
\xi
in an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As a result, the Identity Principle
for polynomials leads to

c_{\alpha}s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\alpha|+1)=0,

for all
|\alpha|\leq|\beta|
.

Consequently, since
s\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{N}
,
the product
s(s-1)\ldots(s-|\alpha|+1)
never vanishes,
and so the coefficients
c_{\alpha}
are forced to be null for any
|\alpha|\leq|\beta|
.
This is in contradiction with (
4.89
), and therefore the proof
of (
4.88
) is complete.

From this, the desired claim in
Proposition
4.18
plainly follows.
∎

Chapter 5
Proof of the main result

This chapter is devoted to the proof of the main result in Theorem
2.1
.
This will be accomplished by an auxiliary result of purely nonlocal type
which will allow us to prescribe an arbitrarily large number of derivatives
at a point for the solution of a fractional equation.

5.1
A result which implies Theorem
2.1

We will use the notation

\Lambda_{-\infty}:=\Lambda_{(-\infty,\dots,-\infty)},

(5.1)

that is we exploit (
2.8
) with
a_{1}:=\dots:=a_{l}:=-\infty
.
This section presents the following statement:

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that

\begin{split}&{\mbox{either there exists\penalty\ $i\in\{1,\dots,M\}$ such that\penalty\ ${\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{i}\neq 0$
and\penalty\ $s_{i}\not\in{\mathbb{N}}$,}}\\
&{\mbox{or there exists\penalty\ $i\in\{1,\dots,l\}$ such that\penalty\ ${\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{i}\neq 0$ and $\alpha_{i}\not\in{\mathbb{N}}$.}}\end{split}

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
with
f\in C^{\ell}\big(\overline{B_{1}^{N}}\big)
. Fixed
\epsilon>0
,
there exist

\begin{split}&u=u_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}\left(B_{1}^{N}\right)\cap C\left(\mathbb{R}^{N}\right),\\
&a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{l})=(a_{1,\epsilon},\dots,a_{l,\epsilon})\in(-\infty,0)^{l},\\
{\mbox{and }}\quad&R=R_{\epsilon}>1\end{split}

such that:

•

for every
h\in\{1,\dots,l\}
and
(x,y,t_{1},\dots,t_{h-1},t_{h+1},\dots,t_{l})

the map
{\mathbb{R}}\ni t_{h}\mapsto u(x,y,t)

belongs to
C^{k_{h},\alpha_{h}}_{-\infty}
,

(5.2)

in the notation of formula (1.4) of
[
CDV18
]
,

•

it holds that

\left\{\begin{matrix}\Lambda_{-\infty}u=0&\mbox{ in }\;B_{1}^{N-l}\times(-1,+\infty)^{l},\\
u(x,y,t)=0&\mbox{ if }\;|(x,y)|\geq R,\end{matrix}\right.

(5.3)

\partial^{k_{h}}_{t_{h}}u(x,y,t)=0\qquad{\mbox{if }}t_{h}\in(-\infty,a_{h}),\qquad{\mbox{for all }}h\in\{1,\dots,l\},

(5.4)

and

\left\|u-f\right\|_{C^{\ell}(B_{1}^{N})}<\epsilon.

(5.5)

The proof of Theorem
5.1
will basically occupy the
rest of this work, and this will lead us to the completion of the
proof of Theorem
2.1
. Indeed, we have that:

Lemma 5.2
.

If the statement of Theorem
5.1
holds true,
then the statement in Theorem
2.1
holds true.

Proof.

Assume that the claims
in Theorem
5.1
are satisfied. Then, by (
5.2
) and (
5.4
),
we are in the position of exploting Lemma A.1 in
[
CDV18
]

and conclude that, in
B_{1}^{N-l}\times(-1,+\infty)^{l}
,

D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}u=D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},-\infty}u,

for every
h\in\{1,\dots,l\}
. This and (
5.3
)
give that

\Lambda_{a}u=\Lambda_{-\infty}u=0\qquad\mbox{ in }\;B_{1}^{N-l}\times(-1,+\infty)^{l}.

(5.6)

We also define

\underline{a}:=\min_{h\in\{1,\dots,l\}}a_{h}

and take
\tau\in C^{\infty}_{0}([-\underline{a}-2,3])
with
\tau=1

in
[-\underline{a}-1,1]
. Let

U(x,y,t):=u(x,y,t)\,\tau(t_{1})\dots\tau(t_{l}).

(5.7)

Our goal is to prove that
U
satisfies the theses of Theorem
2.1
.
To this end, we observe that
u=U
in
B^{N}_{1}
, therefore (
2.12
)
for
U

plainly follows from (
5.5
).

In addition, from (
2.6
), we see that
D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}
at a point
t_{h}\in(-1,1)

only depends on the values of the function
between
a_{h}
and
1
. Since the cutoffs in (
5.7
) do not
alter these values, we see that
D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}U=D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}u

in
B_{1}^{N}
, and accordingly
\Lambda_{a}U=\Lambda_{a}u
in
B_{1}^{N}
.
This and (
5.6
) say that

\Lambda_{a}U=0\qquad{\mbox{in }}B_{1}^{N}.

(5.8)

Also, since
u
in Theorem
5.1
is compactly supported
in the variable
(x,y)
, we see from (
5.7
) that
U

is compactly supported in the variables
(x,y,t)
.
This and (
5.8
) give that (
2.11
) is satisfied by
U

(up to renaming
R
).
∎

5.2
A pivotal span result towards the proof of Theorem
5.1

In what follows, we let
\Lambda_{-\infty}

be as in (
5.1
), we recall the setting in (
2.1
),
and we
use the following multi-indices notations:

\begin{split}&\iota=\left(i,I,\mathfrak{I}\right)=\left(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n},I_{1},\ldots,I_{M},\mathfrak{I}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{I}_{l}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{N}\\
{\mbox{and }}&\partial^{\iota}w=\partial^{i_{1}}_{x_{1}}\ldots\partial^{i_{n}}_{x_{n}}\partial^{I_{1}}_{y_{1}}\ldots\partial^{I_{M}}_{y_{M}}\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}}_{t_{1}}\ldots\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}}_{t_{l}}w.\end{split}

(5.9)

Inspired by Lemma 5 of
[
DSV1
]
,
we consider the span of the derivatives of functions in
\ker\Lambda_{-\infty}
, with derivatives up to a fixed order
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We want to prove that the derivatives of such functions span
a maximal vectorial space.

For this, we denote by
\partial^{K}w(0)

the vector with entries given,
in some prescribed order,
by
\partial^{\iota}w(0)
with
\left|\iota\right|\leq K
.

We notice that

\partial^{K}w(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
for some
K^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

(5.10)

with
K^{\prime}
depending on
K
.

Now, we adopt the notation in formula (1.4) of
[
CDV18
]
,
and
we denote by
\mathcal{A}

the set of all functions
w=w(x,y,t)

such that for all
h\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
and all
(x,y,t_{1},\ldots,t_{h-1},t_{h+1},\ldots,t_{l})\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
,
the map
{\mathbb{R}}\ni t_{h}\mapsto w(x,y,t)
belongs to
C^{\infty}((a_{h},+\infty))\cap C^{k_{h},\alpha_{h}}_{-\infty}
,
and (
5.4
) holds true for some
a_{h}\in(-2,0)
.

We also set

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}:=\Big\{w\in C(\mathbb{R}^{N})\cap C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{N-l})\cap C^{\infty}(\mathcal{N})\cap\mathcal{A},\text{ for some neighborhood
$\mathcal{N}$
of the origin, }\\
\text{ such that }\Lambda_{-\infty}w=0\text{ in }\mathcal{N}\Big\}\end{split}

and, for any
w\in\mathcal{H}
, let
\mathcal{V}_{K}
be the vector space spanned by the vector
\partial^{K}w(0)
.

By (
5.10
), we know that
\mathcal{V}_{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
.
In fact, we show that equality holds in this inclusion, as
stated in the following
1
1

1

Notice that results
analogous to Lemma
5.3

cannot hold for solutions of local operators: for instance,
pure second derivatives of harmonic functions have to satisfy
a linear equation, so they are forced to lie in a proper subspace.
In this sense, results such as Lemma
5.3
here reveal a truly nonlocal
phenomenon.

result:

Lemma 5.3
.

It holds that
\mathcal{V}_{K}=\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
.

The proof of Lemma
5.3
is
by contradiction. Namely, if
\mathcal{V}_{K}
does not exhaust the whole of
\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}
there exists

\theta\in\partial B_{1}^{K^{\prime}}

(5.11)

such that

\mathcal{V}_{K}\subseteq\left\{\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{\prime}}\text{ s.t. }\theta\cdot\zeta=0\right\}.

(5.12)

In coordinates, recalling (
5.9
),
we write
\theta

as
\theta_{\iota}=\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}
,
with
i\in\mathbb{N}^{p_{1}+\dots+p_{n}}
,

I\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{1}+\dots+m_{M}}

and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathbb{N}^{l}
.
We consider

\begin{split}&{\mbox{a multi-index $\overline{I}\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{1}+\dots+m_{M}}$
such that it maximizes\penalty\ $|I|$}}\\
&{\mbox{among all the multi-indexes\penalty\ $(i,I,\mathfrak{I})$
for which\penalty\ $\left|i\right|+\left|I\right|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K$}}\\
&{\mbox{and\penalty\ $\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0$
for some\penalty\ $(i,\mathfrak{I})$.}}\end{split}

(5.13)

Some comments on the setting
in (
5.13
). We stress that, by (
5.11
),
the set
\mathcal{S}

of indexes
I
for which there exist indexes
(i,\mathfrak{I})
such that
|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K

and
\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0
is not empty.
Therefore, since
{\mathcal{S}}
is a finite set,
we can take

S:=\sup_{I\in{\mathcal{S}}}|I|=\max_{I\in{\mathcal{S}}}|I|\in{\mathbb{N}}\cap[0,K].

Hence, we consider a multi-index
\overline{I}
for
which
|\overline{I}|=S
to obtain the
setting in (
5.13
). By construction, we have that

•

|i|+|\overline{I}|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K
,

•

if
|I|>|\overline{I}|
, then
\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}=0
,

•

and there exist multi-indexes
i
and
\mathfrak{I}

such that
\theta_{i,\overline{I},\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0
.

As a variation of the setting in (
5.13
),
we can also consider

\begin{split}&{\mbox{a multi-index $\overline{\mathfrak{I}}\in\mathbb{N}^{l}$
such that it maximizes\penalty\ $|\mathfrak{I}|$}}\\
&{\mbox{among all the multi-indexes\penalty\ $(i,I,\mathfrak{I})$
for which\penalty\ $\left|i\right|+\left|I\right|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K$}}\\
&{\mbox{and\penalty\ $\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0$
for some\penalty\ $(i,I)$.}}\end{split}

(5.14)

In the setting of (
5.13
) and (
5.14
),
we claim that there exists an open set
of
\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}

such that for every
(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,)
in such open set we have that

\begin{split}{\mbox{either }}\qquad&0=\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|=|\overline{I}|}}C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,^{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,^{{I}}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,^{\mathfrak{I}},\qquad{\mbox{ with }}\qquad C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0,\\
{\mbox{or }}\qquad&0=\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|\mathfrak{I}|=|\overline{\mathfrak{I}}|}}C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,^{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,^{{I}}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,^{\mathfrak{I}},\qquad{\mbox{ with }}\qquad C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0.\end{split}

(5.15)

In our framework, the claim in (
5.15
) will be pivotal
towards the completion of the proof of Lemma
5.3
.
Indeed, let us suppose for the moment that (
5.15
)
is established and let us complete the proof of Lemma
5.3

by arguing as follows.

Formula (
5.15
) says that
\theta\cdot\partial^{K}w(0)

is a polynomial which vanishes for any triple
(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,)

in an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}+\ldots+m_{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{l}
.
Hence, using the identity principle of polynomials, we have that each
C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}
is equal to zero
whenever
|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K

and either
|I|=|\overline{I}|
(if the first identity in (
5.15
)
holds true) or
|\mathfrak{I}|=|\overline{\mathfrak{I}}|

(if the second identity in (
5.15
)
holds true). Then, since
C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\neq 0
,
we conclude that each
\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}
is zero
as long as either
|I|=|\overline{I}|
(in the first case)
or
|\mathfrak{I}|=|\overline{\mathfrak{I}}|

(in the second case), but this contradicts either
the definition of
\overline{I}

in (
5.13
) (in the first case)
or the definition of
\overline{\mathfrak{I}}

in (
5.14
) (in the second case). This would therefore complete the proof
of Lemma
5.3
.

In view of the discussion above,
it remains to prove (
5.15
).
To this end, we distinguish the following four
cases:

1.

there exist
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and

j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
such that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\neq 0
and
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\neq 0
,

2.

there exist
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and

h\in\{1,\dots,l\}
such that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\neq 0
and
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\neq 0
,

3.

we have that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}=\dots={\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{n}=0
,
and there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
such that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\neq 0
,

4.

we have that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}=\dots={\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{n}=0
,
and there exists
h\in\{1,\dots,l\}
such that
{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\neq 0
.

Notice that cases
1
and
3

deal with the case in which space-fractional diffusion is present
(and in case
1
one also has classical
derivatives, while in case
3

the classical derivatives are absent).

Similarly, cases
2
and
4

deal with the case in which time-fractional diffusion is present
(and in case
2
one also has classical
derivatives, while in case
4

the classical derivatives are absent).

Of course, the case in which both space- and time-fractional diffusion occur is already comprised by the
previous cases (namely, it is comprised in
both cases
1
and
2

if classical derivatives are also present,
and in both cases
3

and
4
if classical derivatives are absent).

Proof of (
5.15
), case
1
.

For any
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,M\right\}
we denote by
\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}

the first eigenfunction for
(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}

vanishing outside
B_{1}^{m_{j}}
given in Corollary
4.9
.
We normalize it such that
\|\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}})}=1
,
and we write
\lambda_{\star,j}\in(0,+\infty)
to indicate the corresponding first eigenvalue
(which now depends on
s_{j}
), namely we write

\begin{cases}(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}=\lambda_{\star,j}\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}&\quad\text{in}\,B_{1}^{m_{j}},\\
\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}=0&\quad\text{in}\,\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\setminus\overline{B_{1}^{m_{j}}}.\end{cases}

(5.16)

Up to reordering the variables and/or
taking the operators to the other side of the equation,
given the assumptions of case
1
,
we can suppose that

{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}\neq 0

(5.17)

and

{\mbox{${\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{M}>0$}}.

(5.18)

In view of (
5.17
), we can define

R:=\left(\frac{1}{|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}|}\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}|\lambda_{\star,j}}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}|\right)\right)^{1/|r_{1}|}.

(5.19)

Now, we fix two sets of free parameters

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{1}\in(R+1,R+2)^{p_{1}},\ldots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{n}\in(R+1,R+2)^{p_{n}},

(5.20)

and

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,1}\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right),\dots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,l}\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right).

(5.21)

We also set

\lambda_{j}:=\lambda_{\star,j}
for
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,M-1\right\}
,

(5.22)

where
\lambda_{\star,j}
is defined as in (
5.16
), and

\lambda_{M}\,:=\,\frac{1}{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{M}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{j}\right|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j}^{r_{j}}}-\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{j}}-\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\right).

(5.23)

Notice that this definition is well-posed, thanks to (
5.18
).
In addition, from (
5.20
), we can write
\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j}=(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j1},\dots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{jp_{j}})
, and
we know that
\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j\ell}>R+1

for any
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,p_{j}\}
.
Therefore,

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j}^{r_{j}}=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j1}^{r_{j1}}\dots\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{jp_{j}}^{r_{jp_{j}}}\geq 0.

(5.24)

From this, (
5.19
) and (
5.21
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{j}\right|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j}^{r_{j}}}\geq\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}\right|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{1}^{r_{1}}\geq\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}\right|(R+1)^{|r_{1}|}>\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{1}\right|R^{|r_{1}|}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}|\lambda_{j}}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}|\geq\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{j}}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h},

and consequently, by (
5.23
),

\lambda_{M}>0.

(5.25)

We also set

\omega_{j}:=\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{if }\,j=1,\dots,M-1,\\
\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\star,M}^{1/2s_{M}}}{\lambda_{M}^{1/2s_{M}}}&\quad\text{if }\,j=M.\end{cases}

(5.26)

Notice that this definition is well-posed, thanks to (
5.25
).
In addition,
by (
5.16
), we have that,
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
, the functions

\phi_{j}\left(y_{j}\right):=\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}\left(\frac{y_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\right)

(5.27)

are eigenfunctions of
(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}
in
B_{\omega_{j}}^{m_{j}}

with external homogenous Dirichlet boundary condition,
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}
:
namely, we can rewrite (
5.16
) as

\begin{cases}(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}{\phi}_{j}=\lambda_{j}{\phi}_{j}&\quad\text{in}\,B_{\omega_{j}}^{m_{j}},\\
{\phi}_{j}=0&\quad\text{in}\,\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\setminus\overline{B_{\omega_{j}}^{m_{j}}}.\end{cases}

(5.28)

Now, we define

\psi_{\star,h}(t_{h}):=E_{\alpha_{h},1}(t_{h}^{\alpha_{h}}),

(5.29)

where
E_{\alpha_{h},1}
denotes the Mittag-Leffler function
with parameters
\alpha:=\alpha_{h}
and
\beta:=1
as defined
in (
3.1
).

Moreover,
we consider
a_{h}\in(-2,0)
, for every
h=1,\dots,l
,
to be chosen appropriately in what follows
(the precise choice will be performed in (
5.48
)),
and, recalling (
5.21
),
we let

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}:=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{1/{\alpha_{h}}},

(5.30)

and we define

\psi_{h}(t_{h}):=\psi_{\star,h}\big(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}(t_{h}-a_{h})\big)=E_{\alpha_{h},1}\big(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}(t_{h}-a_{h})^{\alpha_{h}}\big).

(5.31)

We point out that, thanks to Lemma
3.2
, the function in (
5.31
), solves

\begin{cases}D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}\psi_{h}(t_{h})=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\psi_{h}(t_{h})&\quad\text{in }\,(a_{h},+\infty),\\
\psi_{h}(a_{h})=1,\\
\partial^{m}_{t_{h}}\psi_{h}(a_{h})=0&\quad\text{for every }\,m\in\{1,\dots,[\alpha_{h}]\}.\end{cases}

(5.32)

Moreover, for any
h\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, we define

\psi^{\star}_{h}(t_{h}):=\begin{cases}\psi_{h}(t_{h})\qquad\text{ if }\,t_{h}\in[a_{h},+\infty)\\
1\qquad\qquad\text{ if }\,t_{h}\in(-\infty,a_{h}).\end{cases}

(5.33)

Thanks to (
5.32
) and Lemma A.3 in
[
CDV18
]
applied here with
b:=a_{h}
,
a:=-\infty
,
u:=\psi_{h}
,
u_{\star}:=\psi^{\star}_{h}
, we have that
\psi^{\star}_{h}\in C^{k_{h},\alpha_{h}}_{-\infty}
, and

D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},-\infty}\psi_{h}^{\star}(t_{h})=D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},a_{h}}\psi_{h}(t_{h})=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\psi_{h}(t_{h})=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\psi_{h}^{\star}(t_{h})\,\text{ in every interval }\,I\Subset(a_{h},+\infty).

(5.34)

We observe that the setting in (
5.33
) is compatible with the ones in (
5.2
) and (
5.4
) .

From (
3.1
) and (
5.31
), we see that

\psi_{h}(t_{h})=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,(t_{h}-a_{h})^{\alpha_{h}j}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)}}.

Consequently, for every
{\mathfrak{I}_{h}}\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have that

\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{h}}_{t_{h}}\psi_{h}(t_{h})=\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,\alpha_{h}j(\alpha_{h}j-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)(t_{h}-a_{h})^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)}}.

(5.35)

Now, we define, for any
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
,

\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}}{\left|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\right|}\quad\text{ if }{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\neq 0,\\
1\quad\text{ if }{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}=0.\end{cases}

We notice that

\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\neq 0
for all
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
,

(5.36)

and

{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}=|{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}|.

(5.37)

Now, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we consider
the multi-index
r_{i}=(r_{i1},\dots,r_{ip_{i}})\in\mathbb{N}^{p_{i}}
.
This multi-index acts on
\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}}
,
whose variables are denoted by
x_{i}=(x_{i1},\dots,x_{ip_{i}})\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{i}}
.
We let
\overline{v}_{i1}
be the solution of
the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\partial^{r_{i1}}_{x_{i1}}\overline{v}_{i1}=-\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\overline{v}_{i1}\\
\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{x_{i1}}\overline{v}_{i1}\left(0\right)=1\quad\text{ for every }\beta_{1}\leq r_{i1}-1.\end{cases}

(5.38)

We notice that
the solution of the Cauchy problem in (
5.38
)
exists at least in a neighborhood of the origin of the form

[-\rho_{i1},\rho_{i1}]
for a suitable
\rho_{i1}>0
.

Moreover, if
p_{i}\geq 2
,
for any
\ell\in\{2,\dots,p_{i}\}
, we consider
the solution of the following Cauchy problem:

\begin{cases}\partial^{r_{i\ell}}_{x_{i\ell}}\overline{v}_{i\ell}=\overline{v}_{i\ell}\\
\partial^{\beta_{\ell}}_{x_{i\ell}}\overline{v}_{i\ell}\left(0\right)=1\quad\text{ for every }\beta_{\ell}\leq r_{i\ell}-1.\end{cases}

(5.39)

As above, these solutions
are well-defined at least in a neighborhood of the origin of the form
[-\rho_{i\ell},\rho_{i\ell}]
,
for a suitable
\rho_{i\ell}>0
.

Then, we define

\overline{\rho}_{i}:=\min\{\rho_{i1},\dots,\rho_{ip_{i}}\}=\min_{\ell\in\{1,\dots,p_{i}\}}\rho_{i\ell}.

In this way, for every
x_{i}=(x_{i1},\dots,x_{ip_{i}})\in B^{p_{i}}_{\overline{\rho}_{i}}
,
we set

\overline{v}_{i}(x_{i}):=\overline{v}_{i1}(x_{i1})\ldots\overline{v}_{i{p_{i}}}(x_{ip_{i}}).

(5.40)

By (
5.38
) and (
5.39
), we have that

\begin{cases}\partial^{r_{i}}_{x_{i}}\overline{v}_{i}=-\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\overline{v}_{i}\\
\\
\partial^{\beta}_{x_{i}}\overline{v}_{i}\left(0\right)=1\quad\begin{matrix}&\text{ for every $\beta=(\beta_{1},\dots\beta_{p_{i}})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{p_{i}}$}\\
&\text{ such that\penalty\ $\beta_{\ell}\leq r_{i\ell}-1$ for each\penalty\ $\ell\in\{1,\dots,p_{i}\}$.}\end{matrix}\end{cases}

(5.41)

Now, we define

\rho:=\min\{\overline{\rho}_{1},\dots\overline{\rho}_{n}\}=\min_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\overline{\rho}_{i}.

We take

\overline{\tau}\in C_{0}^{\infty}\left(B^{p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}}_{\rho/(R+2)}\right),

with
\overline{\tau}=1
in
B^{p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}}_{\rho/(2(R+2))}
, and,
for every
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p_{1}}\times\dots\times{\mathbb{R}}^{p_{n}}
, we set

\tau_{1}\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\right):=\overline{\tau}\left(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{1}\otimes x_{1},\ldots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{n}\otimes x_{n}\right).

(5.42)

We recall that the free parameters
\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{1},\dots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{n}

have been introduced in (
5.20
), and we have used here the notation

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}\otimes x_{i}=(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i1},\dots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{ip_{i}})\otimes(x_{i1},\dots,x_{ip_{i}}):=(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i1}x_{i1},\dots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{ip_{i}}x_{ip_{i}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p_{i}},

for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

We also set, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

v_{i}\left(x_{i}\right):=\overline{v}_{i}\left(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}\otimes x_{i}\right).

(5.43)

We point out that if
x_{i}\in B^{p_{i}}_{\overline{\rho}_{i}/(R+2)}
we have that

|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}\otimes x_{i}|^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p_{i}}(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i\ell}x_{i\ell})^{2}\leq(R+2)^{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{p_{i}}x_{i\ell}^{2}<\overline{\rho}_{i}^{2},

thanks to (
5.20
), and therefore the setting in (
5.43
)
is well-defined for every
x_{i}\in B^{p_{i}}_{\overline{\rho}_{i}/(R+2)}
.

Recalling (
5.41
) and (
5.43
), we see that, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

\partial_{x_{i}}^{r_{i}}v_{i}(x_{i})=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}\partial_{x_{i}}^{r_{i}}\overline{v}_{i}\left(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}\otimes x_{i}\right)=-\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}\overline{v}_{i}\left(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}\otimes x_{i}\right)=-\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}{v}_{i}(x_{i}).

(5.44)

We take
e_{1},\ldots,e_{M}
, with

e_{j}\in\partial B_{\omega_{j}}^{m_{j}},

(5.45)

and we introduce an additional set of free parameters

Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{M}
with

Y_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\qquad{\mbox{
and }}\qquad e_{j}\cdot Y_{j}<0.

(5.46)

We let
\epsilon>0
, to be taken small
possibly depending on the free parameters
e_{j}
,
Y_{j}
and
\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}
,
and we define

\begin{split}w\left(x,y,t\right):=&\tau_{1}\left(x\right)v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\cdot\ldots\cdot v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\cdot\ldots\cdot\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\\
&\times\psi^{\star}_{1}(t_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\psi^{\star}_{l}(t_{l}),\end{split}

(5.47)

where the setting in (
5.27
), (
5.33
), (
5.42
) and (
5.43
) has been exploited.

We also notice that
w\in C\left(\mathbb{R}^{N}\right)\cap C_{0}\left(\mathbb{R}^{N-l}\right)\cap\mathcal{A}
. Moreover,
if

a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{l}):=\left(-\frac{\epsilon}{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{1}},\dots,-\frac{\epsilon}{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{l}}\right)\in(-\infty,0)^{l}

(5.48)

and
(x,y)
is sufficiently close to the origin
and
t\in(a_{1},+\infty)\times\dots\times(a_{l},+\infty)
, we have that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{-\infty}w\left(x,y,t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\partial^{r_{i}}_{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},-\infty}\right)w\left(x,y,t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{i-1}\left(x_{i-1}\right)\partial^{r_{i}}_{x_{i}}v_{i}\left(x_{i}\right)v_{i+1}\left(x_{i+1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\psi^{\star}_{1}\left(t_{1}\right)\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}\left(t_{l}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{M}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{j-1}\left(y_{j-1}+e_{j-1}+\epsilon Y_{j-1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times(-\Delta)^{s_{j}}_{y_{j}}\phi_{j}\left(y_{j}+e_{j}+\epsilon Y_{j}\right)\phi_{j+1}\left(y_{j+1}+e_{j+1}+\epsilon Y_{j+1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\psi^{\star}_{1}\left(t_{1}\right)\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}\left(t_{l}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\psi^{\star}_{1}\left(t_{1}\right)\ldots\psi^{\star}_{h-1}\left(t_{h-1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times D^{\alpha_{h}}_{t_{h},-\infty}\psi^{\star}_{h}\left(t_{h}\right)\psi^{\star}_{h+1}(t_{h+1})\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}\left(t_{l}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\psi^{\star}_{1}(t_{1})\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}(t_{l})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{M}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{j}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\psi^{\star}_{1}(t_{1})\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}(t_{l})

\displaystyle+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}v_{1}\left(x_{1}\right)\ldots v_{n}\left(x_{n}\right)\phi_{1}\left(y_{1}+e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\phi_{M}\left(y_{M}+e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\psi^{\star}_{1}(t_{1})\ldots\psi^{\star}_{l}(t_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}\overline{{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}}_{i}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{j}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\right)w(x,y,t),

thanks to (
5.28
), (
5.34
) and (
5.44
)
.

Consequently, making use of (
5.22
), (
5.23
) and (
5.37
),
if
(x,y)
lies near the origin and

,
we have that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{-\infty}w\left(x,y,t\right)=\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{j}+{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{M}\lambda_{M}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\right)w(x,y,t)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}|{\mbox{\large{\wedn{a}}}}_{i}|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{i}^{r_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{j}\lambda_{\star,j}+{\mbox{\large{\wedn{b}}}}_{M}\lambda_{M}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}{\mbox{\large{\wedn{c}}}}\,_{h}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\right)w(x,y,t)=0.

This says that
w\in\mathcal{H}
. Thus, in light of (
5.12
) we have that

0=\theta\cdot\partial^{K}w\left(0\right)=\sum_{\left|\iota\right|\leq K}{\theta_{\iota}\partial^{\iota}w\left(0\right)}=\sum_{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\partial_{x}^{i}\partial_{y}^{I}\partial_{t}^{\mathfrak{I}}w\left(0\right).

(5.49)

Now, we recall (
5.40
) and we claim that,
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,p_{j}\}
and any
i_{j\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

\partial^{i_{j\ell}}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j\ell}(0)\neq 0.

(5.50)

We prove it by induction over
i_{j\ell}
. Indeed, if

i_{j\ell}\in\left\{0,\ldots,r_{j\ell}-1\right\}
, then
the initial condition in (
5.38
) (if
\ell=1
)
or (
5.39
) (if
\ell\geq 2
) gives that
\partial^{i_{j\ell}}_{x_{i\ell}}\overline{v}_{i\ell}\left(0\right)=1
, and
so (
5.50
)
is true in this case.

To perform the inductive step,
let us now
suppose that the claim in (
5.50
)
still holds for all
i_{j\ell}\in\left\{0,\ldots,i_{0}\right\}

for some
i_{0}
such that
i_{0}\geq r_{j\ell}-1
.
Then, using the equation in (
5.38
) (if
\ell=1
)
or in (
5.39
) (if
\ell\geq 2
), we have that

\partial^{i_{0}+1}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j}=\partial^{i_{0}+1-r_{j\ell}}_{x_{j\ell}}\partial^{r_{j\ell}}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j}=-\tilde{a}_{j}\partial^{i_{0}+1-r_{j\ell}}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j},

(5.51)

with

\tilde{a}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\overline{a}_{j}&{\mbox{ if }}\ell=1,\\
-1&{\mbox{ if }}\ell\geq 2.\end{cases}

Notice that
\tilde{a}_{j}\neq 0
, in view of (
5.36
),
and
\partial^{i_{0}+1-r_{j\ell}}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j}\left(0\right)\neq 0
,
by the inductive assumption. These considerations and (
5.51
)
give that
\partial^{i_{0}+1}_{x_{j\ell}}\overline{v}_{j}\left(0\right)\neq 0
,
and this proves (
5.50
).

Now, using (
5.40
) and (
5.50
) we have that,
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and any
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{p_{j}}
,

\partial^{i_{j}}_{x_{j}}\overline{v}_{j}(0)\neq 0.

This, (
5.20
) and the computation in (
5.44
) give that,
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and any
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{p_{j}}
,

\partial^{i_{j}}_{x_{j}}{v}_{j}(0)=\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{j}^{i_{j}}\partial^{i_{j}}_{x_{j}}\overline{v}_{j}(0)\neq 0.

(5.52)

We also notice that, in light of (
5.33
), (
5.47
) and (
5.49
),

\begin{split}&0=\sum_{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\partial^{i_{1}}_{x_{1}}{v}_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{i_{n}}_{x_{n}}{v}_{n}(0)\,\partial_{y_{1}}^{I_{1}}\phi_{1}\left(e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\partial_{y_{M}}^{I_{M}}\phi_{M}\left(e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}}_{t_{1}}\psi_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}}\psi_{l}(0).\end{split}

(5.53)

Now, by (
5.27
)
and Proposition
4.10

(applied to

,

,

),
we see that, for any
j=1,\ldots,M
,

\begin{split}\omega_{j}^{|I_{j}|}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{|I_{j}|-s_{j}}\partial_{y_{j}}^{I_{j}}\phi_{j}\left(e_{j}+\epsilon Y_{j}\right)\;=\;&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{|I_{j}|-s_{j}}\partial_{y_{j}}^{I_{j}}\tilde{\phi}_{\star,j}\left(\frac{e_{j}+\epsilon Y_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\right)\\
=\;&\kappa_{j}\frac{e_{j}^{I_{j}}}{\omega_{j}^{|I_{j}|}}\left(-\frac{e_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\cdot\frac{Y_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\right)_{+}^{s_{j}-|I_{j}|},\end{split}

(5.54)

with
\kappa_{j}\neq 0
, in the sense of distributions (in the coordinates
Y_{j}
).

Moreover, using (
5.35
) and (
5.48
),
it follows that

\displaystyle\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{h}}_{t_{h}}\psi_{h}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,\alpha_{h}j(\alpha_{h}j-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)(0-a_{h})^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,\alpha_{h}j(\alpha_{h}j-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)\,{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)\;{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}}^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,\alpha_{h}j(\alpha_{h}j-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)\,{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)\;{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}}^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}}.

Accordingly, recalling (
5.30
), we find that

\begin{split}&\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\epsilon^{\mathfrak{I}_{h}-\alpha_{h}}\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{h}}_{t_{h}}\psi_{h}(0)=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}{\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}^{j}\,\alpha_{h}j(\alpha_{h}j-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)\,{\epsilon}^{\alpha_{h}(j-1)}}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}j+1\right)\;{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}}^{\alpha_{h}j-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}}\\
&\qquad={\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{\star,h}\,\alpha_{h}(\alpha_{h}-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}+1\right)\;\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}^{\alpha_{h}-\mathfrak{I}_{h}}}}={\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,_{h}^{\mathfrak{I}_{h}}\,\alpha_{h}(\alpha_{h}-1)\dots(\alpha_{h}-\mathfrak{I}_{h}+1)}{\Gamma\left(\alpha_{h}+1\right)}}.\end{split}

(5.55)

Also, recalling (

), we can write (

\begin{split}&0=\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|\leq|\overline{I}|}}\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\partial^{i_{1}}_{x_{1}}{v}_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{i_{n}}_{x_{n}}{v}_{n}(0)\,\partial_{y_{1}}^{I_{1}}\phi_{1}\left(e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\partial_{y_{M}}^{I_{M}}\phi_{M}\left(e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}}_{t_{1}}\psi_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}}_{t_{l}}\psi_{l}(0).\end{split}

(5.56)

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\qquad\times\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}}_{t_{1}}\psi_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}}_{t_{l}}\psi_{l}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|\leq|\overline{I}|}}\epsilon^{|\overline{I}|-|I|}\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\partial^{i_{1}}_{x_{1}}{v}_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{i_{n}}_{x_{n}}{v}_{n}(0)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\epsilon^{|I_{1}|-s_{1}}\partial_{y_{1}}^{I_{1}}\phi_{1}\left(e_{1}+\epsilon Y_{1}\right)\ldots\epsilon^{|I_{M}|-s_{M}}\partial_{y_{M}}^{I_{M}}\phi_{M}\left(e_{M}+\epsilon Y_{M}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\epsilon^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}-\alpha_{1}}\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{1}}_{t_{1}}\psi_{1}(0)\ldots\epsilon^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}-\alpha_{l}}\partial^{\mathfrak{I}_{l}}_{t_{l}}\psi_{l}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|=|\overline{I}|}}\tilde{C}_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\partial^{i_{1}}_{x_{1}}{v}_{1}(0)\ldots\partial^{i_{n}}_{x_{n}}{v}_{n}(0)

Hence, recalling (

\begin{split}0&\,=\,\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|=|\overline{I}|}}C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\theta_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n},I_{1},\dots,I_{M},\mathfrak{I}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{I}_{l}}\;\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{1}^{i_{1}}\ldots\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,_{n}^{i_{n}}\\

&\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{{|i|+|I|+|\mathfrak{I}|\leq K}\atop{|I|=|\overline{I}|}}C_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\,\theta_{i,I,\mathfrak{I}}\;\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,^{i}\,e^{I}\,\left(-e_{1}\cdot Y_{1}\right)_{+}^{-|I_{1}|}\ldots\left(-e_{M}\cdot Y_{M}\right)_{+}^{-|I_{M}|}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,^{\mathfrak{I}},\end{split}

(5.57)

for a suitable

.

We observe that the equality in (

(\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{x}}}}\,,Y,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{t}}}}\,)

Now, we take new free parameters,
\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{1},\ldots,\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{M}

\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{j}}\setminus\{0\}
, and we

e_{j}:=\frac{\omega_{j}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}}{|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}|}\quad{\mbox{ and }}\quad Y_{j}:=-\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}}{|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}|^{2}}.

(5.58)

We stress that the setting in (
5.58
) is compatible with that in (
5.46
), since

e_{j}\cdot Y_{j}=-\frac{\omega_{j}\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}}{|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}|}\cdot\frac{\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}}{|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}|^{2}}=-\frac{\omega_{j}}{|\underline{\mbox{\large{\wedn{y}}}}\,_{j}|}<0,

and hence

Plugging this into formula (
5.57
),
we obtain the first identity in (
5.15
),
as desired.
Hence,
the proof of (
5.15
) in case

is complete.
